About  the  time  our  ancestor  Conrad  Euler  came  to  Philadelphia .with  his
-family  in  September  1729,  something  occur ed  about  that  time  in  the  life,
if  Ben  Franklin  a  printer,  who  later  became  our  ambassador  to  r ranee  during

the  Revolution.

Franklin  operated  a  print  shop  in  Philadelphia,  and  here  are  some  of  the
dates  and  events  during  this  period:

1.  in  Sept  1730  Franklin  married  Deborah  Read

2.  In  Oct  27,  1729  Bust  about  one  month  after  Conrad  arrived  here,  Franklin
ran  an  ad  offering  for  sale  the  followin  g  items  from  his  print  shop  Bibles,
testaments,  Paalters,  psalm  books,  bills  of  lading,  bonds  with  judgement,
common  blank  money  bonds,  bills  of  sale,  powers  of  attorney,  writs,  summons,

promisory  notes.

3.  In  1728  Franklin  got  a  contract  to  print  paper  money  for  the  colony  of
Pennsylvania.

4.  In  1729  Franklin  discussed  in  his  newspaper  the  "  Pennsylvania  Gazette'1
the  Massachusetts  assumbly.

5.  In  1728  Franlin  printed  his  "  Articles  of  belief"

6.  In  1731  Franklin  fathered  a  illegimate  son  named  William

((

EULER ,  EILAR,  ILLER,  ILER ,  OYLER ,  OILER,  A NT  OTHER  SPELLINGS
OF  THIS  FAMILY  IN  AMERICA

Our  ancestor  came  to  America,  with  his  family*  on  the  ship  Allen
on  September  11,1729.  Records  of  the  Church  of  the  Brethren,  show  he
Wns  a  member  of  this  church,  pnd  their  pastor  end  founder  of  the
church,  Alexander  Meek  was  one  of  the  passengers.

Records  show  Conrad  else  signed  the  Oath  of  Allegiance  at  Philadelphia •

Tr  John  S  Eilar  of  Albuquerque  NM ,  Cleo  Griggs  Gilchrist  of  Eoise  Idaho,
and  others  have  done  quite  a  bit  of  research,  on  the  original  family.

What  I  show  here  is  largely  from  their  work,  plus  what  was  done  by
Mrs  Resile  Norman  Schorn  of  Tulsa  Oklahoma.

I  find  on  researching  the  Oyler  name  as  spelled  here,  in  the  telephone
directories  for  all  the  states,  covering  the  principal  cities,  there  are
about  6<0  Cyle^sli sted .  In  addition  I  checked  the  records  of  immigrants
to  Philadelphia  from  1729  to  1R05,  and  there  are  about  R  more  Oylers
cAme  over,  with  spelling  similar  to  ours.

We  feel  that  cur  family  came  from  Maar  parish,  near  Lautejrback,  Hessen
West  Germany.  We  find  about  20  Oylers  listed  in^ a  recent  telephone
directory  from  Lauterhack,  Ned  Oyler  of  Eagle  Colorado,  recently  had
a  friend  of  his  stop  in  Lauterback  and  interview  some  of  the  Oyler s( spelled
Euler  in  Germany).  Church  records  there  indicate  that  the  family  goes
back  to  about  1^00.

There  is  a  crest  of  the  Oylers  on  the  church  wall  in  Maar,  and  I  have
Included  here  further  ahead  a  letter  from  Karl  Euler,  a  retired  Major
in  the  German  army  who  has  been  researching  in  Germany.

There  is  some  confusion  about  the  date  of  birth  from  church  records  in
Maar ,  but  it  arrears  that  Conrad  Oyler  was  born  between  1675  end  1^90,
and  his  father  was  Michael  Cy  ler.

Our  Conrad  brought  with  him  his  wife  Margaret _ ,  have  not  been  able

to  find  her  maiden  name  and  sons  Jacob,  Valentine,  and  daughters
Elizabeth,  Maria  Dorothea,  Catarina,  And  Anna  Barbara.

No  one  has  been  able  to  find  the  sons  listed  in  the  ships  passenger  list,
however  the  «:irls  and  Marvaret  were  listed,  but  later  evidence  shows
when  Valentine  becrun  buylfc^his  brother  and  sisters  interest  inhls  fathers
land  after  his  death  in  1751,  that  the  boys  would  have  been  born  in
Germany,  but  their  names  did  not  appear  on  the  ship  list.

I  have  tried  to  include  family  lines  for  all  Oylers  I  could  ^et  acauainted
with,  from  the  first  family  of  Conrad’s  down  to  the  present  day  Oyler  or
other  family  memver.

In  1970  a  young,  prfd?st  came  and  8  years  ago  it  was  repainted.

The  priest  had  the  foresight  to  photograph  all  the  crests(cq^t  of  arras).
The  crests  were  located  on  the  panels  as  marked  on  the  enclosed  picture
.of  the  interior  of  the  church.  A  colored  enlargedment  will  be  sent  to
me  in  the  future.

The  family  tree  mentioned  above  *  will  be  taken  to  an  Abbey  and  have  a
prfcnt  made  of  it,  since  they  have  the  means  of  doing  this  on  a  large
document,  this  flamily  tree  takes  the  Euler  family  back  to  14  7«

This  friend  brought  me  back  a  book  of  the  history  of  Maar,  but  it  is
in  German.  If  you  feel  that  you  could  get  it  translated ,  maybe  I  could
get  it  zeroxed ,  about  140  pages.  It  tells  how  the  people  would  mortgage
their  land  and  lose  it  to  Heir  Hessen.  It  also  tell  how  each  family
would  give  their  children  portions,  and  in  1706  a  law  was  passed
forbidding  this.  It  was  ignored  until  1714  when  it  was  enforced.  After
this  the  young  people  left  in  large  numbers,  since  they  could  not  own
land  •

I  h^ve  enclosed  a  page  from  a  current  phone  book,  showing  the  Eulers
listed1.

Karl  Euler  is  a  retired  army  major,  his  son  is  an  englls^teacher  at  the
university.  The  last  day  there  my  friend  was  taken  to  visit  Kart  Euler
who  had  done  a  lot  of  research  and  is  thrilled  to  hear  from  us  since  we
are  writing  a  book  on  the  Oyler  family  tree.  Karl  lsualso  writing  a  book
on  theEUler  family  tree,  and  it  lists  4  d  if  f  erent^fl£aving  there  for
America.  He  wants  to  add  into  his  book  what  has  happened  here.

The  priest  is  very  anxious  for  contact  and  by  working  through  this
german  friend  of  mine,  who  will  be  visiting  there  each  year(Maar  area)

I  believe  we  might  have  something.

I  will  send  you  copies  of  the  pictures  thst  are  coming,  plus  snap  shots
taken.

We  are  going  to  Utah  for  a  family  reunion  on  July  18,  1981.  The  priest
will  be  back  on  July  ?7 .  1981  and  we  hope  he  will  get  the  crest  and  famil
tree  to  us  soon  after  that.  I  will  also  send  you  another  group  of  my
family  sheets  soon,  but  I  believe  what  I  have  here  is  worth  waiting  a
little  while,  for  it  to  arrive  from  germany.  I  will  share  it  with  you,
when  your  book  is  finished,  I*m  sure  we  will  want  quite  a  few  of  them.

P.uroperrn  Origins  of  the  Brethren

went  to  America  and  arrived  in  Philadelphia.  At  that  place  A.  M
went  to  meet  him  and  entreated  him  for  God’s  sake  to  forget  and
forgive  what  had  happened  in  Europe ;  to  which  lie  agreed.

George  Adam  Martin.'1

Arrival  in  Pennsylvania

It  was  not  until  1729  that  Mack  led  the  Wittgenstein  group  on  the
long  and  dangerous  ocean  voyage  from  the  Netherlands  to  the  New  WorU
In  1727  Mack  had  sold  his  share  of  the  inheritance  from  his  father-in l»*
fohn  Valentine  Kling  (who  died  in  Schriesheim  in  1714),  while  in
Surhuisterveen.  Neither  of  the  two  Anna  Margaret  Macks  given  on  thr
passenger  list  of  the  / Him  refers  to  Mack’s  wife,  who  died  in  vSeptcmhr
1720,  according  to  the  entry  in  Alexander  Mack  s  Bible,  now  preserved  it
Bridgewater  College,  Bridgewater,  Virginia.

It  is  probable  that  some  of  those  listed  were  not  members  of  the
Brethren  group,  but  merely  passengers  on  the  same  ship.  The  names  hisr
been  edited  in  an  attempt  in  reconstruct  more  accurate  spellings,  smrr  ifc
Ijsts  were  written  bv  English  clerks  who  had  erent  difficulty  spelling
German  names.  Although  there  is  a  less  complete  list  with  actual  sign*
tures,  some  of  the  group  were  illiterate  and  their  names  were  also  written
by  clerks  on  this  list  as  well.

The  following  shows  when  Conrad  and  son  Jacob  acquired  some  land  In
Lancaster  County  Pennsylvania

The  "Pennsylvania  Archives  Series**  volume  xxlv  page  399  show  that  Conrad
and  Jacob  Eyler  each  appeared  on  same  day  and  took  out  warrants  for
tracts  of  land  200  acres  In  Lancaster  County,  3  October  1738,

Dr  Eller’s  personal  search  of  records(1936)  In  recorder  of  deeds  office
York,  Pa  showed  the  land  was  on  a  brach  of  Codorus  Creek,  on  the  West
side  of  the  Susquehanna  river,  then  part  of  Lancaster  County,  now  In
Manhelm  Township,  York  Co  Pa.  The  land  was  near  the  town  of  Hanover  Pa*,’
In  Southwest  part  of  York  Co,  near  the  Pa  and  Maryland  state  line.

Dr  Eller  visited  Hanover  at  that  time  and  received  these  records*

from  ** German  Element  In  York  Co**  by  Dr  Wentz  **  In  the  thriving  town
of  Hanover,  In  173^.  Conrad  Eyler  and  son  Valentine  settled  on  Diggs
tract  receiving  their  warrants  In  1738.

On  the  next  page  Is  a  map  of  the  Conrad  Eller  and  Valentine  Eyler  land

den  USA.  Der  in  meinem  Besitz  befindliche  Stammbaum  wurde  von
meinem  Onkel  in  Maar  undeinem  weiteren  Vetter  Ferdinand  Euler,
damals  als  Pfarrer  in  Erfurt,  nach  Eintragungen  aus  den  Kirchen-
blichern  in  Maar  und  Lauterbach,  sowie  aus  deln  Archiv  der  Freiherrn
Riedesel'in  Lauterbach  und  aus  Ausziigen  aus  dem  Deutschen
G-eschlechterbuch,  Band  98,  im  Jahre  1934  zusammengest ellt  und  lm
Jahre  1946  vom  Sammelarchiv  Iller-Euler  in  Bad-Salzschlirf  im
Entwurf  herausgebracht .  Die  Autoren  hatten  besondere  Schwierig-
keiten  aus  dem  Mangel  an  Auf zeichnungen  aus  dem  30-jahrigen  Kneg
(1618-1648)  und  dem  7-jahrigen  Krieg  (1756-1763)  die  beide  unsere
Heimat  arg  verwiisteten.  Erst  mit  der  EinfM.hrung  der  Kirchen-
biicher  ab  1766  liegen  dann  einwandfreie  Eintragungen  vor.

Da  Sie  aber  so  gute  Angaben  liber  Xhre  Vorfahren  machen  konnten,
konnte  auch  ich  meine  Suchaktion  auf  die  Zeit  zwischen  1670  und
1729  beschranken.  Dabei  stellte  ich  festi  Im  Jahre  1677  war
Michael  Euler  Blirgermeist er  in  Maar,  er  starb  am  22.10.1694  und
war  zweimal  verheiratet  gewesen.  Die  2.  Ehe  mit  einer  Anna  Hel—
fenbein  erfolgte  am  4.  4.  1675  und  erbrachte  5  Kinder,  deren
©rster  Sohn  Konrad  Euler,  geboren  am  4.7.1675  das  weitere  Glied
in  meinem  Stammbaum  darstellt,  und  auch  hier  starb. .Uber  das
G-e bur t s datum  des  Michael  Euler,  sowie  uber  den  Madchennamen .
seiner  ersten  Frau  und  liber  Kinder  aus  dieser  Ehe  liegen  keine
Angaben  vor.  Es  sind  in  diesem  Zsfitabschnitt  auch  keine  Aussagsn
liber  einen  weiteren  Konrad  Euler  und  auch  liber  dessen  Sohn
Valentin  Euler,  geboren  1722,  vorhanden.  Nun  weiB  ich  nicht,
sind  die  Autoren  damals,  urn  den  Stammbaum  libersichtlicher  zu
halten,  an  diesem  Namen  vorbeigegangen,  was  ich  vermute,  denn
daB  Kinder  aus  der  ersten  Ehe  nicht  genannt  wurden  sagt  nicht
aus,  daB  keine  da  waren,  Oder  es  hat  die  damaligen  Sucher  nicht
interessiert .  Es  ist  also  notwendig,  diesen  Michael  Euler  einmal
naher  zu  betrachten  und  auch  etwas  liber  seine  erste  Ehe  zu  er-
fahren.

Ich  werde  dies  tun  und  Ihnen  dann  gegebenenf alls  darliber  berich-
ten.  Ich  bin  fest  davon  liberzeugt ,  daB  Ihre  Vorfahren  in  diese
Llicke  gehoren.

Pfarrer  Schnecker  aus  Maar,  der  sich  auch  meiner  Meinung  an-
schlieBt,  hat  Ihnen  ja  inzwischen  eine  Ablichtung  meines  Stamm-
baum-Entwurf s  zugesandt.

'»  Lauterbach,  4.  November  1981

bitte  wenden

I

Ich  hoffe.Sie  kommen  damit  zurecht.

—  *.  >

Machen  Sie  doch  Ihrer  Urheimat  mal  einen  Besuch.  Ich  wiirde  mich
sehr  freuen  und  Sie  winweisen.

Sie  sind  jederzeit  herztlch  eingeladen.

Mit  freundlichen  G-ruBen
Ihr  -  entfernter  Vetter

This  letter  translated  from  German  in  December  19pl.  the  letter  was
written  November  4,  19^1  by  Karl  Eulejr  of  ftauterback  Germaney

Re:  Your  letter  of  November  9*  1991  Family  tree  Euler  from  Maar.

I  * m  glad  to  discover  a  distant  cousin  in  the  USA .  The  family  tree  in
my  possesion  was  compiled  by  my  uncle  in  Maar  and  my  cousis  Ferdinand
Euler,  was  was  a  parson  in  Erfurt  from  church  records  in  Maar  and
Lauterbach,  as  well  as  from  Baron  Riedesel  of  Lauterbach  archives, and
frov  German  Geneology  Boob.  Voulme  9$  which  was  compiled  in  193^  and
Published  in  194£  by  Iller-Euler  in  Bad -Salzschlert .  The  authors  had
special  difficulty  in  finding  information  during  the  time  of  the  30  year
ar (161P-164P )  and  the  7  year  war ( 175^-17^3 ) »  both  of  which  destroyed  much
of  our  county. Clear  records  are  only  available  from  17^6  when  the  shurch
started  "keeping  records.

However  since  your  inf ormati t>fc  for  the  period  ld70-17?9  is  so  good,  I
was  se-gee  able  to  concentrate  on  that  time.  This  is  what  I  found:

Michael  Euler  was  mayor  of  Maar  in  1 677,  he  died  October  PP,  1694,  and
was  married  twice.  The  second  marriage  to  Anna  Helf enbeln ' on  April  4,  1^75
pruduced  5  children,  whose  first  son  Konrad  Euler  born  Jtf’y  4,  1^75  and
deceased  here0  In  the  family  link  I  have  followed  there  Is  no  information
on  Michael  Euler* s  birth  date,  nor  maiden  name  of  his  first  wife  or
whether  there  were  any  children  from  -the  first  marriage.

Also,  in  this  period  there  is  no  information  Re:  Konrad  Euler  or  his  son
Valentine  Euler,  bonn  17??  available.  I  have  no  idea  if  this  information
was  deliberately  bypassed,  which  I  suspect,  the  fact  that  no  children
rom  the  first  marriage  does  not  mean  that  there  were  none,  or  it  was
of  no  interest  to  the  family  tree  searchers.  It  is  therefore  necessary
to  further  investigate  this  Michael  E>0_er  and  to  learn  more  about  his
first  marriage.

I  will  undertake  this  and  report  to  you  further.  I*m  convinced  that  your
ancestors  belong  in  this  space.

Rev  Schnecker  from  Maar,  who  is  of  the  same  opinion  has  in  the  meantime
went  you  a  copy  of  my  family  tree.

I  hope  this  has  been  of  some  help  to  you.

^hy  not  v?  sit  us  some  time?  I*d  be  delighted  to  see  you  and  show  you
ground.  •.  \

Pest  Regards

Karl  Euler,  Major  a.E.

In  meiner  Linie  aus  Maar  sind  nur  2  Manner,  der  Valentin  Euler
geboren  am  22.3.1826  und  der  Konrad  Euler  geboren  am  9.12.1832,
die  nachweBBlich  nach  Amerika  ausgewandert  sind.  Aber  wie  und
wann  sie  dort  angekommen  und  wo  sie  gelebt  haben  ist  Leider  nicht
bekannt •  Ich  babe  aber  die  lo  Hamen  an  das  Kirchenamt  in  Maar  und
zur  Ahnenforschung  weitergegeben  und  sofern  icb  d^ariiber  Neues
erfahre,  werde  icb  selbstvers tandlich  sofort  an  Dicb  schreiben.

Fur  heute  mochte  icb  Dir  und  Deinen  Angeborigen  meine  allerbesten
Wiinsche  und  GriiBe  zum  Weihnachtsf est  senden  und  auch  fur  das
Neue  Jabr  viel  Erfolg  und  vor  allem  beste  Gesundheit.

"engere  Zusammenarbe ' Staateh~mlt  dem  Ziele
zur  Erbaltung  des  Friedens  und  der  Frsiheit  sind  die  weiteren
Wiinsche  fur  1983-  Wir,  hart  an  der  Grenze  zwiscben  Ost  und  West,
und  die  Verwandten  meiner  Frau  in  Thuringen,  ostwarts  der  Grenze,
beheimatet,  tragen  doch  den  grofiten  TeiL.  der  Last  fur  das’  ge~

Herrn  W.  Monroe  Oyler!

Dear  Monroe,

I  still  owe  you  a  reply  to  your  letter  of  September  20,  1982,
in  which  you  as ted  about  the  10  Euler,  Eyler  or  Oyler,  who
arrived  in  Philadelphia  between  1729  and  1804.  1  am  only  able
to  tell  you  that  these  men  are  not  mentioned  among  my  descen¬
dants.  In  my  familyline  from  Maar  only  2  men,  Valentin  EulSr ,
born  March  22nd,  1826,  and  Konrad  Euler,  born  December  9th,
1832,  have  been  traced  back,  and  that  they  immigrated  to  Ameri¬
ca.  But  how,  and  when  they  arrived  there,  and  where  they
lived-  afterwards ,  is  unfortunately  not  Known.  I  haw  parsed  ~on
the  ten  names  to  the  parish  in  Maar  and  also  to  the  genealo¬
gical  research,  and  should  I  receive  any  news,  I  would  cer¬
tainly  write_  to  you.  3*

%

Today,  I  would  like  to  send  to  you  and  your  family  my  best

wishes  for  the  Christmas  Season  and  much  success  and  good

health  for  the  New  Year.  Also  hone  for  1983  that  there  will

be  progress  in  politics  for  a  united  Europe  and  close  rela-

*

tionship  with  the  United  States,  with  the  goal  to  preserve
peace  and  personal  freedom.  We,  close  to  the  border  of  East
and  West,  and  my  wife’s  relatives  in  ’fhuringen  east  erf  the-
border,  carry  the  largest  part  of  the  burden  for  the  device d
homeland.  But  with  faith  in  Cod  and  our  own  determination,
we  will  solve  any  future  problems.  May  Cod  give  us  enough
strength.

Sincere  greetings  in  true  friendship,
your  distant  cousin.

LUe  have  not  corrected  obvious  errors  in  spelling  of  either  Christian
or  surnames,  e.  c.  Bingimon,  Cristofer,  micle,  Partrick,  but  have  recorded
the  names  exactly  as  they  appeared  to  us.  Be  sure  to  look  for  all  possible
spellings  of  the  names  you  seek.

Unfortunately  we  were  not  consistent  in  our  listing  of  free  non-whites.
They  are  entered  as  they  were  recorded  in  the  schedules,  and  for  example,
Barbarah  Free  Negress  and  Biddy  are  under  the  B's,  but  Free  Ben  is  with  the
F's.  Of  course,  when  the  free  non-white  had  a  surname,  the  listing  is
alphabetical  for  that  surname.

Marie  Dorothea  Euler  wee  daughter  of  Conrad  Euler.  She  was  horn  stout

1713  In  Gerrany  nnd  came  to  Philadelphia  in  1779  with  her  family.

She  married  Frederick  Henry  Gelw3oks  10  April  1733  in  Providence  Pa,

was  baptized  at  Trappe  in  February  1734,  but  the  second,  third,  and

fourth  children  were  baptized  at  Muddy  Creek  in  northern  Lancaster
County  between  1736  and  1739. 6  Apparently  at  least  some  of  the
Barnitz  family  and  some  of  the  Euler  family  spent  time  at  Muddy
Creek  as  well,  but  Conrad  Euler  warranted  land  in  Conewago  on
October  3  1738, 7  and  the  Barnitz  and  Gelwicks  families  also

settled  there,  perhaps  in  the  spring  of  1740.  Conewago  was  a
settlement  in  a  badly  disputed  area  claimed  by  both  Pennsylvania  and
Maryland.  Leonhardt  Barnitz  and  his  sons  presently  left  for  lork
which  was  laid  out  in  1741,  but  Conrad  Euler  and  his  sons  sett  ed  on
his  200-acre  tract  to  which  there  were  no  patent  deeds. intil  oag

the  Euler  land,8  and  he  also  held  a,  tract  in  Manheim  Township  wh  ch
he  warranted  June  8,  1762  and  surveyed  May  20,  1766,  the  teres
paid  to  1  March  1744,  possibly  the  date  he  took  it  up.  This
adjoined  his  first,  though  not  contiguously.

His  account  book  shows  us  that  Frederick  Gelwicks  was
frequently  involved  in  the  affairs  of  the  church,  for  he  had
regular  dealings  with  several  Lutheran  pastors  and  collected  the
offerings  of  members  or  saw  that  they  were  paid.  Moreover  a
number  of  times  he  sold  people  a  Namcnbuch ,  the  primer,  used  to
teach  reading  in  the  Pennsylvania  German  schools.  They  may  have
been  left  from  the  time  he  was  teaching.

In  1756  Frederick  Gelwicks  was  elected  treasurer  of  York
County,  which  had  been  erected  in  1749.  He  was  the  first  German
to  hold  the  office,  which  demanded  obviously  the  ability  to  read
and  write  and  to  communicate  in  English.  In  the  account  book
there  is  laid  a  memorandum  to  George  Stevenson,  a  prominent  early
York  County  official  --  register,  recorder,  prothonotary ,  clerk  of
the  courts,  and  chief  ranger  from  1749  to  1764  --  trying  to  swap
support  for  Stevenson  for  sheriff  in  return  for  his  endorsement  of
Gelwicks  for  commissioner  in  1765.  Nothing  came  of  this  attempt
apparently. *  *

*

Undoubtedly,  Gelwicks  and  his  family  also  farmed  the
147  1/2  acres  which  he  patented  as  "White  Oak  Level"  on
28  August  1761. 12  There  are  references  to  farming  activities  in
the  account  book.  These  include  the  harvesting  of  crops  --  a  chore
in  which  women  and  girls  also  labored  --  and  the  sale  of  farm-produced
commodities.  A  day's  wage  for  farm  work  appears  to  have  been  about
2  shillings,  6  pence  --  about  what  Gelwicks  charged  for  the  labor  on
a  pair  of  shoes.

Frederick  Gelwicks  was  also  a  shoemaker.  This  was
undoubtedly  a  part-time  occupation  filled  in  seasons  when  the  farm
work  was  not  demanding.  As  a  shoemaker,  Gelwicks  went  to  his
neighbors  to  mend  old  shoes  and  make  new  ones.  Sometimes  the
customer  provided  the  leather  and  other  components,  but  when
Gelwicks  provided  it,  the  price  of  women's  and  children's  shoes  was
less  than  an  adult  male's,  since  the  amount  of  leather  used  and  not
the  labor  invested  was  the  variable  in  determining  the  cost.  Whereas
a  man's  shoe  might  cost  eight  shillings,  a  woman's  would  range
around  6  and  a  child's  from  about  3  to  7 .  Occasionally  Gelwicks
made  boots  and  a  kind  of  houseshoe  or  slipper,  especially  for  women;
and  tantalizing  references  to  caps  and  a  pocketbook  suggest  that  he
was  capable  of  more.  Shoes  were  one  item  of  clothing  which  the
family  could  not  produce  itself  and  they  were  expensive  enough  that
they  were  mended  and  mended  again.

Frederick  also  created  a  still  at  the  large  spring  on  his
farm  before  1760,  when  this  account  book  was  opened.  Like  many
other  Pennsylvania  Germans,  Gelwicks  learned  that  grain  was  worth
more  in  the  form  of  liquor  and  that  it  was  easily  marketable  that

x

I

L  ,t|

'

-

U  ^rn  l  '  II

way.  The  variety  of  beverages  he  produced  suggest  that  he  was
fairly  advanced  in  his  skill  --  and  the  large  quantities  the
neighbors  bought  are  evidence  that  only  the  temperance  movement
of  the  1830 's  partly  diminished  the  drinking  of  home-made  rye
whiskey  among  the  Pennsylvania  Dutch.  Among  the  purchasers  of
Gelwicks's  product  were  persons  who  held  tavern  licenses  and  could
sell  whiskey  by  the  glass,  but  others  purchased  for  domestic  use,
such  as  at  house  or  barn  raisings  and  funerals,  and  for  consumption
in  the  harvest  fields.  The  various  beverages  are  listed  in  the
glossary  and  defined  there.  Naturally  simple  sweet  cider  cost  only
a  few  pence  a  quart,  but  Gelwicks's  Gassel  (a  cordial?)  was  the  most
expensive  item  produced  at  a  shilling,  6  pence  a  quart.  Brandy,

rye  and  applejack  all  cost  about  9  or  10  pence  a  quart.

As  has  been  suggested,  account  books  reveal  a  great  deal
about  life  in  the  community  from  which  they  came.  Among  others,
the  Gelwicks  account  book  mentions  gambling,  lotteries,  the  use  of
snuff  and  fairs  --  all  of  which  became  sins  of  one  degree  or  another
in  the  nineteenth  century  --  the  terms  of  an  apprentice  and  what  he
cost  his  master,  the  fees  charged  a  boarder,  the  variety  of
currencies  in  use,  and  the  sale  of  six  ducks  to  Pastor  Baugher  a  few
days  before  Christmas  in  1768,  some  of  which  no  doubt  ended  up  on
the  parsonage  table  for  Christmas  dinner  that  year.

Perhaps  the  most  interesting  page  in  the  book  is  the
agreement  concluded  between  Gelwicks  and  PNeter  Keplinger  in  April
1780  renting  the  farm  to  Keplinger,  who  subsequently  bought  it  on
19  August  1780.  Schott  Scheier  are  mentioned  there,  a  rare  form  of
grain  storage  known  as  barracks  in  English;  as  is  the  Bastert ,  a
colorful  Pennsylvania  German  term  for  poor,  scrubby,  wooded  pasture.

During  the  .American  Revolution,  Frederick  Gelwicks  was  a
loyal  American.  With  his  son  ( Chari es  he  was  on  the  York  County
Committee  of  Safety.  On  8  November  1779  he  was  appointed  a
commissioner1 to  collect  arms  and  accoutrements,  blankets,  woolen  and
linsey-wool sy  cloth,  linens  and  shoes  and  stockings  for  the  army  from
inhabitants  who  had  not  signed  the  oath  of  allegiance  and  abjuration
or  who  had  aided  the^enemv.  Several  sons  served  in  the
revolutionary  army.1,5

About  the  time  of  the  Gelwicks-Kepl inger  lease,  Frederick
and  Maria  Dorothea  Gelwicks  must  have  moved  into  Hanover,  the  town
established  in  1763  nearby  (and  mentioned  in  the  account  in  1764  as
the  stadt  Hanover),  where  he  purchased  a  lot  on  Baltimore  Street.

His  son,  Charles  had  been  living  there  as  a  shoemaker  and  later
tavern  keeper  since  1764;  and  Peter,  another  son,  occupied  a  house
almost  directly  across  York  Street  from  Charles's  which  his  father
owned.  Frederick  Gelwicks  died  in  Hanover  on  8  April  1783,  his  wife

/»

xi

,

(

following  him  on  15  May  1785.^  They  were  probably  buried  in
Hanover's  only  cemetery  at  the  time,  that  at  the  German  Reformed
Church.

Frederick  Gelwicks  wrote  a  will  on  20  March  1783,  stating
that  he  was  "aged  and  sickly,"  in  which  he  left  his  widow  the  right
to  his  dwelling  and  such  furnishings  as  she  desired.  Upon  her
death  the  entire  estate  was  to  be  converted  to  cash  and  divided
among  the  children,  but  oldest  son  John  Frederick  was  to  receive  in
addition,  as  his  primogeniture  benefit,  the  things  that  had  already
been  given  him,  all  of  which  are  carefully  recorded  in  the  records
that  follow.  The  executors  were  his  son  Nicholas  and  his  son-in-law
Philip  Meyer.

An  inventory  was  made  5  May  1783,  but  since  neither  shoe¬
making  equipment  nor  the  still  are  mentioned,  it  is  obvious  that
Frederick  Gelwicks  was  retired.  The  distillery  had  been  sold  to
Nicholas,  as  several  references  in  the  accounts  below  make  clear.

The  widow  selected  her  basic  needs,  including  the  Bible  and  a
"litle  flowert  chest"  worth  7/6.  Final  account  in  the  estate  was
filed  in  May  1786.  The  house  and  lot  in  Hanover  were  sold  to

ii.  Elias  Daniel,  born  about  1720,  lived  at  Baltimore,

iii.  John  George  Carl,  bom  14  August  1722  at  "Falken-

Maria  Dorothea  Euler,  the  wife  of  Frederick  Gelwicks,  was
a  daughter  of  Conrad  and  Anna  Margaretha  Euler,  who  do  not  seem  to
be  on  any  ship  list.  Their  family  were:^

i.  Valentine,  1717-1790,  farmer  of  Manheim  Township
and  later  Menallen  Township,  now  in  Adams  County.

ii.  Jacob,  carpenter,  of  Manheim  Township  in  1763.

iii.  Anna  Barbara,  married  at  Conewago  19  September  1740,
Frederick  Kreuter,  in  1763  of  Frederick  County,
Virginia .

iv.  Elisabeth,  married  "Lawrence  Schepp;"  in  1764  on
the  Atkin  River  in  North  Carolina.

v.  Catharina,  married  at  Muddy  Creek  9  March  1736,

Jacob  Verdrigs,  i.e.,  Verdriess,  in  1763  of
Frederick  County,  Virginia.

vi.  Maria  Dorothea,  married  Friedrich  Henrich  Gelwicks.

Frederick  Gelwicks’s  accounts  are  in  a  book  31  1/2  cm  by
19  cm,  which  now  contains  105  pages  (numbered  in  square  brackets
below),  although  some  others  have  obviously  been  cut  out  at  one
time  or  another.  The  book  was  intended  for  use  by  the  son  Georg
Pari  (Charles),  for  whom  its  title  page  was  lettered,  probably  by
scFnene'r  in  1760,  but  the  reverse  of  the  title  page  says
"Friederich  Gelwicks's  writing  book,"  and  the  remainder  seems  to  be
in  his  hand  except  for  a  few  entries  in  another,  «htch  (to  judge
from  the  signatures  of  the  heirs  in  the  book)  is  m  fact  Charles  s.

The  book  came  into  the  possession  of  Henry  Wi£t,  a  1°<Tal  JV** Tarriaee
in  Hanover,  who  was  a  descendant  of  Charles  through  his  f^st  marriage
Henrv  Wirt's  wife  was  a  Forney,  and  upon  her  death  this  (and  “a"/

documents)  were  taken  to  the  bam  of  the  Forney  house
on  Frederick  Street  in  Hanover,  where  G.  Benjamin  and  Helen  Forney.
Howells  found  it  many  years  later.  Mrs.  Howells  has  very  kindly
brought  the  book  to  our  attention,  allowed  copies  of  it  to  be  made,
and  now  given  permission  for  its  publication.

given  names  which  occur  in  this  account;  and  a  se

(1746-1806)  was  a  son  of  Valentine,  whom  see,  who  eventually

~  ~  &(o

(14  December  1717-10,  April  1790)  was  a  son  of  Conrad  Euler  and
therefore  a  brother-in-law  of  Frederick  Gelwicks.  He  purchased
his  father's  farm  (or  more  accurately  the  rights  to  it)  from  the
other  heirs  and  apparently  lived  on  part  of  it  until  about  1  6,

when  he  moved  to  the  neighborhood  of  Arendtsville  in  Adams  Coi  nty
and  turned  part  of  the  farm  over  to  his  son  Johannes  and  sold  the
rest  to  Christian  Herr.  His  first  wife  was  Apollonia,  but  his
will  identifies  a  wife  Susanna,  who  may  hatfe  been  the  mother  of
some  of  his  children.  There  is  a  reference  to  whiskey  for  the
funeral"  in  Valentine’s  account,  in  January  1762,  which  may  have
been  for  Apol Ionia's  burial,  although  he  did  have  a  wife  mentioned
in  September.  His  children:

'

Joel  Wright,
Early
Quaker
School
Master

Joel  Wright  was  the  schoolmaster  of  the  Friends  School  at  Pipe  Creek
Settlement  during  the  last  30  years  of  the  18th  century.  His  services
were  sought  for  land  measurement  and  the  location  of  boundaries
when  the  school  was  not  in  session  The  village  of  Petersburg,  Pa.  has
been  given  as  the  place  of  his  birth  but  another  source  gives  Gettysburg.

When  the  surveyors  Jeremiah  Dixon  and  Charles  Mason  appeared  in
the  vicinty  to  determine  the  Pennsylvania-Maryland  boundary,  Joel  was
entering  his  teens.  It  was  August  1764,  when  the  "Stargazers"  as  Mason
and  Dixon  called  themselves  were  nearby.  Their  journal  states  that  they
crossed  Piney  Run,  a  tributary  of  the  Monocacy  which  empties  into  the
great  River  Potowmak  — .  On  August  8th,  they  recorded  that  "  —  a  great
storm  of  Thunder,  Lightening,  Hail  with  intermixt  pieces  of  Ice  came."

Jeremiah  Dixon  was  a  Friend  and  Joel  Wright's  parents  were  also
Quakers.  The  Wrights  home  would  have  been  open  to  the  strangers
from  England  and  possibly  Dixon  stayed  with  them  as  he  traveled
through  this  area.  As  a  youngster,  Joel  had  shown  an  aptitude  for
figures  and  his  parents  had  encouraged  his  efforts.  The  appearance  of
the  eminent  mathematicians  may  have  given  the  boy  the  required  in¬
spiration  to  place  his  feet  on  the  road  to  his  future  vocation.

At  Pipe  Creek  Settlement  in  1771  there  were  but  four  houses  within
easy  reach  of  the  new  Meetinghouse.  A  tribe  of  Indians  had  at  one  time
maintained  a  semi-permanent  place  of  abode  near  the  Little  Pipe  Creek,
in  the  valley.  After  the  signing  of  the  Treaty  of  Lancaster  on  July  4,
1744,  they  had  migrated  to  the  Ohio  Country.

It  was  decided  to  open  a  school  in  1771,  in  Pipe  Creek,  under  the
supervision  of  the  Friends.  Joel  Wright  was  chosen  to  be  the  head¬
master.  About  this  time,  Joel  met  and  married  Elizabeth  Farquhar,  one
of  the  daughters  of  William  and  Ann  Farquhar,  the  pioneer  settlers  of
the  1730's.

^ew§  ^IvetteE

^Historical  §ocietyC of
Carroll  County^  Md.  Inc.

Volume  37  Number  One  March,  1986

Valentine  Eyler  produced  a  certificate  drawn  up  in
Frederick  County,  Maryland  and  signed  by  Joel  Wright
and  15  others,  neighbors  of  Eyler,  stating  'that  his
general  character  had  not  that  we  know  of,  been  charged
with  anything  unfavorable,  except  his  Attachment  in  the
British  Interest  in  the  late  war,  for  which  he  suffered  im
prisonment  and  had  his  estate  confiscated

Remote  settlements  of  Quakers  were  visited  by  both  Joel  and
Elizabeth  Wright  during  the  years  following  the  War  of  Independence.
As  a  recorded  minister  she  "traveled  in  the  ministry."  Journeys  required
careful  planning  because  of  the  hazards  to  be  met  with  in  unknown
country.  One  day's  journey  on  horseback,  during  this  period  might  be
20  miles.  With  good  conditions,  30  miles  per  day  might,  on  occasion,  be
achieved.  On  a  typical  journey,  one  to  western  Pennsylvania  for  exam¬
ple,  careful  planning  was  necessary  so  that  the  end  of  the  day  would
find  them  near  hospitable  shelter.  A  tradition  about  Elizabeth  Wright's
travels  concerns  the  many  sugar  maple  trees  to  be  seen  around  Union
Bridge.  The  original  seedlings  were  brought  back  by  Elizabeth  Wright  in
her  saddlebags  from  a  trip  to  the  Redstone  settlement  in  the  far  west  of
Pennsylvania.

During  the  summer  of  1788,  Wright  made  his  first  extended  journey
into  what  was  then  referred  to  as  Northwest  Territory.  By  the  late
1 780's,  many  white  people  were  venturing  into  the  territory  The  tribes
became  uneasy  and  eventually  warfare  with  the  United  States  took
place.  Joel  Wright  went  to  study  conditions  and  returned  with  a  report
to  the  Baltimore  Yearly  Meeting  of  Friends.  During  the  next  decade,
several  more  trips  westward  to  the  Ohio  country  were  made.  He  ex¬
amined  the  valleys  of  the  Little  Miami,  Great  Miami,  Scioto  and  Musk¬
ingum.  In  1798,  he  led  a  small  group  of  concerned  persons  to  meet  with
leaders  of  the  Wyandot  Indians,  and  advise  them,  for  their  future
welfare  This  journey  and  visit  to  the  Wyandot  tribe  was  made  under
the  auspices  of  Baltimore  Yearly  Meeting  of  the  Society  of  Friends.

A  lad  named  John  Elgar  attended  the  Pipe  Creek  School  during  the
1 790's.  Fifty  years  later,  he  wrote  about  his  boyhood.  His  brief
autobiography  ends  with  the  following:

"...and  in  no  spot  have  I  seen,  though  I  have  traveled  far
and  long,  is  she  so  lovely  as  mid  the  hills  and  valleys  and
streams  of  Pike  Creek...

The  Friends  School  of  Pipe  Creek  Settlement  acquired  an  excellent
reputation  and  children  were  sent  from  Fredericktown,  Westminster
and  Taneytown.  They  boarded  with  the  Wright's  or  in  neighboring
/^TorrVes.

Joel's  sagacity  was  recognized  in  1774  when  he  was  appointed  Clerk
of  the  Meeting.  In  1776,  the  new  administration  in  Annapolis  appointed
him  a  justice  of  the  peace  During  this  period  in  Maryland  history  a
j  justice  of  the  peace  functioned  as  a  local  judge  and  the  person  to  whom
j  the  people  nearby  looked  to  for  guidance,  especially  when  the  advice

*  needed  concerned  the  new  state  and  its  functions.  One  case  history  can

be  retold  here  for  its  general  interest.  The  brothers  Valentine  andJgcob
1  Eyler  of  Pipe  Creek  would  not  sign  the  document  sent  around  among
J  the  people  requesting  them  to  affirm  or  swear  allegiance  to  the  State  of
/ 1  Maryland.  They  were  sentenced  to  a  term  in  jail  in  Fredericktown.  The

county  court  then  levied  a  fine.  By  1780-81,  the  authorities  began  ap¬
propriating  the  property  owned  by  the  brothers.  A  treaty  ending  the  war
was  signed  and  still  they  held  out,  stating  that  the  request  was  an  infr¬
ingement  upon  their  rights  as  free  men.  Finally,  they  decided  to  move  to
upper  Canada.  The  Archives  of  the  Province  of  Ontario  possess  the
document:

[This  essay  has  been  condensed  to  conform  with  space  limitations  ]

If  you  the  reader,  would  like  to  read  about  other  early  residents  in
Carroll  County,  be  kind  enough  to  inform  a  staff  member  of  the
Historical  Society.

Amos  Davidson

I  have  received  2  letters  from  Carol  Usa  of  PO  Box  66,  Somers  Wis
53171.  She  was  referred  to  me  by  her  cousin  Kenneth  Ghher ,  Carol
is  a  descendant  of  Jacob  Her,  Eyler  Oyler ,  son  of  our  immigrant
Conrad.  Jacob  was  brother  of  my  ancestor  Valentine.

Her  line  goes  as  follows:

Corrad  Euler  1690-1751

Jacob  Iler  17l8-ca  1770

Conrad  Iler  Sr  17^+9-1816

Conrad  Iler  Jr  1779-1850

Mary  Ellen  Iler  Engle  Thnnfiorst  1835-1900  ?0/r

Peter1  Cornelius  Thunhorst  1873-1928  '7o^1L

Chester  Leonard  Thunhorst  1902-1976

Carol  A  nn  Thunhorst  Usa  1938-  7*i^

One  of  Carolfs  letters  dated  March  15?  1985  reads  as  fallows:

Dear  Mr  Oyler:  Sorry  I've  taken  so  long  to  answer  your  letter  of  the
7th  of  February.  My  daughter  was  married  February  16  and

my  mother  inlaw  has  been  bery  ill,  so  we'v  been  busy  and  I  haven  t
had  nr  ch  time  letely  to  think  about  Genealogy.

I'd  be  glad  to  exchange  info.  Please  tell  me  about  Kenneth  had  sent  you  ?
so  I  don't  recopy  the  same  info.  Mary  Ellen  Iler  Engel  Thunhorst  was
my  rr eat  grandmother.  I  can  send&ou  the  line  from  John  Henry  Thunhorst
,  her  husband  to  me  if  you  like.  I'm  still  trying  to  piece  together  the
Iler  inf or  that  Ke  nneth  sent  me  but  c  an  send  what  I  have  gleamed  from
it  so  far®.

Did  Kenneth  give  you  info  on  her  childr  en  from  David  Engle?  I  have  it
also.  I  had  been  unable  to  find  anything  on  Mary  Ellen  Iler  for  a  long
time  until  Kenneth  found  me,  I  am  really  appreciative  for  his  help  and
the  contacts  through  the  Montgomery  Co  Qen,  Soc.  of  which  I  am  a
charter  member.

How  are  you  related?  Please  send  your  line,  I'm  interested  to  see  it.

Was  your  family  around  to  know  the  Thurnhorsts?  I  was  born  1938  in
Racine  wis  after  my  father  moved  North  off  the  farm  in  Montgonery  C0.
Rountree  Twp  near  St  John's  Lutheran  church  on  Witt  Rd.  Bo  you  live
near  the  county  line (Montgomery  and  Christian)  I'll  ge  looking  forward
you  your  letter.

(I  wrote  her  on  3^20/85  and  sent  my  ot n  line  showing  that  we  connect
back  early,  as  our  twwoancestors  Jacob  born  1718  and  Valentine  born
1722  were  brothers)

page  2  from  Carol  Ann  Usa

/  06

Her  letter  to  me  on  April  11,  1985  reads:

Dpar  Mr  Oyler:  Received  your  letter  and  history.  Here  are  family  group
sheets  to  John  Henry  Thunhorst  my  great  grandfather.  As  you  can  see
I  have  my  great  grandmother,  Mary  Ellen  born  10  April  1838,  Obits,  and
gravestone  says  the  same.  She  may  have  changed  the  year  of  her  virth
to  be  younger,  as  women  do,  but  I  believe  April  is  the  real  no  nth  of
her  birth.  Kenneth  has  a  type  written  letter  from  th?  minister  of
Rocky  Hill,  now  Grace  Church  but  I  believe  the  ink  may  have  faded  on
the  original  records  which  would  explain  the  flifference.

MayyEllen’s  gravestone  is  behind  David  Engles’ z  one  row  and  to  the  left.
We  are  hoping  to  have  David's  stone  raised  by  now,  its  is  shaped  like  so(
(she  draws  a  picture  of  the  stone)  John  Henry  Thunforst  is  on  front,

Mary  Ellen  on  left  side  and  his  second  wife  on  the  right  side.

I’m  enclosing  a  picture  of  a  picture  I  have  on  BHywwall.  It  was  in

VALENTINE  OILER

came  from  Canada  to  Ohio  in  1796,  was  a  Tory,  and  had  to
leave  his  native  State,  Maryland,  during  Revolutionary  war  and  fly
to  the  English  dominions.  We  find  that  in  the  tax  duplicate  for  the

year  1798,  of  Dayton  Township,

the  miller  of  Daniel  C.  Cooper.  So  it  is  evident  that' he  was  bm#
at  an  earlier  date.  He  finally  settled  in  Section  21,  on  the
"Woodman  farm",  and  raised  a  family.  He  was  the  grandfather  of
D.  W.  Pottle,  and  his  youngest  son,  Samuel,  died  near  Hagerstown,

Ind.,  in  1675*

These  facts  came  from  some  papers  left  by  Peter  Lemon,  who  had
collected  Material  with  a  view  of  writing  a  history  of  Mad  River  Twp.

Kemp  Schoolhouse  built  in  the  1815-  This  was  done  by  subscription.
Samuel  Qyler  subscribing  $1.  25.

Dear  Mr.  Oyler,

Your  letter  of  April  10,  1982  that  you  addressed  to  me
went  to  my  sister's  address  in  Royal  Oak,  Michigan.  I
am  not  sure  how  you  got  that  address,  but  it  was  sent
to  me  and  I  will  try  to  answer  it.  Sorry  it  has  taken
so  long.

I  met  Earl  Oyler,  a  music  teacher,  from  Taylorville,
Illinois.  He  came  to  our  concert  in  Pana,  Illinois.

I  see  that  we  are  related  so  I  should  call  you  "Cousin".

The  Conrad  Iller  came  over  on  the  Ship  Allen  in  1729
and  landed  in  Pennsylvania.  I  have  a  copy  of  that  list
in  the  Her  Book  that  Dr.  John  S.  Eilar  of  Albuquerque,
New  Mexico  had  published.  Enclosed  is  my  listing  of  the
family.  I  am  the  ninth  generation  in  this  country  and
five  generations  are  buried  within  three  blocks  at  the
place  Jacob  Iler  settled  in  1808,  now  Harrow,  Ontario,
Canada.  There  are  about  30  different  ways  to  spell  our
name  and  this  makes  it  difficult,  like  many  others,  to
do  research  in  some  areas.

I  appreciated  your  writing  and  thanks  for  the  information
you  sent.  Earl  wrote  out  some  information  for  me  too.

My  glee  club  has  sung  in  Decatur  in  the  past  also.

Conductor,  Moody  Men's  Glee  Club

sometime  and  Earl,  if  this  could  be  arranged  -  perhaps  a
Saturday.

father  Solomon  Iler  was  not  on  your  list  and  I  may  have  made
the  mistake  myself.

work  here  at  Moody,  and  then  I*m  Church  Historian  at  my

going  on  weekends,  and  then  taking  a  week  here  and  there  to

celebrated  their  13&  wedding  anniversary  January  1st.  My
Mother  died  August  9*  19&9*

I  have  information  where  there  was  a  Dwight  Moody  Eiler  down  in
the  Tower  Hill,  Illinois  area  and  I’ve  been  trying  to  find  out
why  he  had  this  name  -  his  parents  could  have  known  Moody  or
accepted  the  Lord  during  his  evangelistic  ministries.  Who  knows?
I  would  like  to  know  anyway.

There  are  four  of  us  in  our  family:

My  older  sister,  Nola  Jean,  Mrs.  L»  Russell  Polk,  lives  in
Dryden,  Michigan.  She  had  three  boys  and  a  girl  and  they
are  all  married  living  inRochester,  Ortonville  and  Lapper,
Michigan.

My  brother,  Ronald  Bruce,  is  married  to  Thelma  Sweetland,
and  they  have  two  daughters,  both  nurses,  and  they  have
three  children,  Shari,  who  lives  in  Portland,  Oregon,  and
Bonnie ,  Mrs.  Gilbert  N.  Branson,  2  girls,  living  in  San
Leandro,  CA.  Shari  and  her  husband.  Rev.  D.  Larry  Rydman,
are  the  ones  in  Portland*  He  pastors  the  Oak  Grove  Baptist.

My  sister  Ruth  Earlene  Tier,  is  not  married  and  lives  in
Royal  Oak,  Michigan,  just  a  mile  from  my  folks.

Dad  will  be  87  in  July  -  25 »  -  and  he  is  an  amazing  person  -
runs  the  place  and  could  run  circles  around  anyone  —  includ¬
ing  myself.

This  coming  April  I  will  be  here  25  years.  It  hardly  seems
possible.  But  I  do  enjoy  my  work  here  and  with  the  glee  club.

Sorry  to  have  been  so  wordie  at  this  time*

I  often  think  I  should  put  in  writing  an  article  about  myself
for  the  historical  records  too.  My  Dad  began  writing  things
this  Christmas  time  about  his  first  Christmas,  he  has  a  fire

he  remembers  seeing  his  first  car,  talking  over  the  first  phone
in  that  area,  etc.,  etc*

said  something  in  German.

Dear  Monroe,

Sorry  to  be  so  late  in  responding  but  I  have  been  terribly
busy  and  just  didn't  have  time  to  get  at  it.

I've  enclosed  some  photocopies  of  Dr.  John  S.  Eilar's  book
and  these  are  the  only  dates  I  have  and  you  can  glean  what
you  need  from  them.

Didn't  realize  there  were  so  many  reunions  going  on  with
the  Ilers/Oylers/Etc.  I  attended  a  Jones  Reunion  in  Grafton,

WV  in  August.  He  was  born  the  same  year  George  Washington
was  (1732).  His  daughter  and  son  were  captured  by  the  Wyan¬
dot  Indians  when  she  was  13  and  he  was  11.  She  was  a  prisoner
for  10  years  and  her  brother  escaped  after  5  years.  She,

Mary  Jones,  was  my  GGG  Grandmother.  Her  granddaughter  married
my  Great  Grandfather  Solomon  Her,  Elizabeth  Malott.  Mary  got
to  Detroit,  Married  Lt.  Peter  Malott,  settled  in  Kingsville,
Ontario  in  1798  (Ilers  were  there  in  1808)  and  in  1817  after
Peter  died,  she  and  her  young  son,  Peter  who  was  13,  took  a
boat  across  Lake  Erie  to  Sandusky,  Ohio  and  walked  the  Indians
Trails  back  to  her  home  and  saw  her  parents  for  the  first  in
40  years.  She  stayed  a  couple  of  months  and  walked  back  again.
She  began  walking  May  29  and  arrived  June  16,  and  that  is  quite
a  jaunt  -  no  motels,  restaurants,  wilderness,  mountains,  streams,
wow,  that  is  really  something.  It  was  the  first  reunion  in  71
years  of  this  family.  She  and  her  husband  are  buried  7  miles
from  my  Dad's  place  in  Harrow,  Ontario,  where  the  Ilers  settled
in  1808.

Genealogical  Sketches

The  great  Gettysburg  reunion

By  WILDAQ.  COOPER

Breeze-Courier  writer

The  June  25,  1913,  edition  of  the
‘Daily  Courier’  gives  an  account  of
the  nine  Civil  War  soldiers  of  Chris¬
tian  County,  planning  to  attend  the
50th  reunion  of  the  battle  of  Gettys¬
burg.

County  Clerk  Henry  J.  Burke  this
morning  received  nine  different
checks  for  $10  each  from  Brig.-Gen.
F.  S.  Dickson,  of  Springfield,  which
will  be  turned  over  to  the  nine  old  sol¬
diers  who  will  attend  the  battle  of
Gettysburg  reunion  at  Gettysburg.

This  $10  is  for  spending  money,  and
does  not  include  car  fare  and  berths
in  Pullman  sleepers,  which  are  also
furnished  free.

Below  we  give  a  sample  of  one  of
the  checks,  which  is  as  follows:

Springfield,  111.  June  28,  T3,  Spring-
field  Marine  Bank,  Pay  to  County
Clerk  of  Christian  County  or  order
$10.  Moses  R.  Witzel,  Participant,
Battle  of  Gettysburg,  Stonington,  Ill.

Signed-F.  S.  Dickson,  Brig.-Gen.,
The  Adjutant  General,  Chief  of  Staff.

erywhere  retain  the  old  shutters

of  many  minie  balls.  The  Gettysburg
of  today  has  not  changed  so  very
much.  The  town  has  grown  larger.

It  will  be  fifty  years  next  Tuesday  Handsome  new  buildings  hav$  sprung

since  the  great  three-days  battle  be¬
gan  at  Gettysburg,  and  it  has  been
that’  long  since  the  Christian  county
veterans  last  saw  the  battlefield.

Every  hotel  is  booked  to  capacity.
The  largest  in  the  town  is  the  Eagle
Hotel,  which  is  the  hub  of  Gettysburg.

All  leave  here,  Friday  morning  on
the  B.  &  O.  for  the  big  reunion  on  the
Gettysburg  battlefield,  which  will  be
held  there  all  rtext  week.  They  have
been  informed  that  after  they  reach
Cincinnati  a  Pullman  sleeper  will  be
furnished  them  for  the  balance  of  the
journey.  Some  of  them  wore  the  blue
and  Some  the  grey  during  that
memorable  conflict.  The  state  of  Il¬
linois,  Pennsylvania  and  Uncle  Sam
together  have  extended  Jx>th  the  boys

forded  in  the  hotel  life  of  the  town.
They  are  installing  fifty  private  baths
and  making  other  improvements.  Se¬
ven  famous  old  heroes  will  stop  at  the
Eagle.  There  are  two  other  hotels  in
Gettysburg.

The  fact  that  many  of  the  Gettys¬
burg  battle  veterans  will  not  be  able
to  leave  their  homes  because  of  ad¬
vanced  age  or  illness  will  present
many  pathetic  incidents.  Men  who
are  not  able  to  take  care  of  them¬
selves  are  discouraged  in  their  at-

Breeze  Courier,  it  was  stated  that  the
Angletons  were  , descendants  of  the
Youngs  and  Angletons.  It  should  have
read  .  that  the  Angletons  were
descendants  of  the  Angletons  and  Ma¬
sons.

James  Monroe  Oyler,  born  in  1843,  my  grandfather  was  a  son
of  Daniel  and  Jemima  Smith  Oyler.  He  was  born  In  Dkalb  Co  Ala.

After  the  Civil  war  he  and  his  brothers  Smith  and  Andrew  came  to
Christian  ^o  Ill,  between  I865  to  lPb9«

My  grandf nther  James  married  Jlary  Elizabeth  Watts..in  I87?  on  her
sisterlnlaws  famr  Bast  of  Sharpsburg,  Mrs  Harvey'Watts.

My  grandfather  owned  80  acres  of  land  3  and  %  mile  Nolth  of  Tayl orville
Ill,  which  I  believe  he  bought  about  1882.  ^ iv

My  grandparents  had  5  children.  Aunt  Bertha,  who/married  Elmer  Harmon,

Lee  Vaughan,  Uncle  Walter  who  married  Ethel  Irwin,  and  4unt  Pearl  who
married  Ambrose  Ban  Ahman,  than  after  divorce  marr iedCalvin  Li'

On  January  16,  ^37^  s^e  was

Taylorville.  CMrT?  Oyler  passed
away  November  5,  1925.  Mrs.

Oyler  is  the  mother  oF  five  chil¬
dren:  Mrs.  Elmer  Harmon,  Mrs.

ville,  and  Walter  Oyler  of  Wil-

grandchildren,  34  great  grand¬
children  and  one  great  great
grandchild.

Mr.  and  Mrs.  Lloyd  Harmon,  822  West  Vine  Street  will  be  honor¬
ed  at  open  house,  Sunday,  afternoon,  October  31,  in  the  home  of
their  son-in-law  and  daughter,  Mr.  and  Mrs.  Russell  Hubbartt,  502
Kenton  Boulevard,  in  celebration  of  their  golden  wedding  anni¬
versary.  They  extend  a  cordial  invitation  to  the  public  to  call  from
2  o’clock  throughout  ,the  afternoon.  (S.  &  L.  Studio)

Mr.  And  Mrs.  Lloyd  Harmon  To  Mark
50th  Wedding  Date  At  Open  House

Mr.  and  Mrs.  Lloyd  Hannon,
822  West  Vine  Street,  will  cele¬
brate  their  golden  wedding  an¬
niversary  on  Sunday,  Oct.  31,  in
the  home  of  their  daughter  and
son-in-law,  Mr.  and  Mrs.  Russell
Hubbartt,  502  Kenton  Boulevard,
with  an  open  house  to  begin  at
2  o’clock  in  the  afternoon.

Mr.  Harmon  and  Miss  Fern
Mil9lagle  were  married,  October
26,  1915  at  Clayton,  Mo.,  by  the
Rev.  Walter  M.  Langtry,  Presby¬
terian  minister  of  Clayton.  They
resided  on  a  farm  east  of  Morri-
sonville  for  25  years,  then  moved
to  UhiA,  city,  where  they  lived  at
902  East  Thompson  Street,  prior
to  occupancy  of  their  present
homo  on  West  Vine,  several
years  ago.  Mr.  Harmon  retired
from  farming  last  year,  but  he
still  maintains  an  active  interest
and  makes  several  trips  to  the
farm  each  week.  N

Mr.  and  Mrs.  Harmon  are  the

WREATHA  NATTERMAN  RETIRES  HER

E(®v#n

(Photof  by  Virginia  Cowell.)

Wreatha  Natterman  retires
after  64  years  in  business

Mrs.  Ernest  (Wreatha)  Natterman,
well-known  resident  of  this  city,  has
announced  her  retirement.

Mrs.  Natterman  has  been  in  busi¬
ness  since  1922  where  she  started  her
beauty  salon  in  January  of  1922.  The
Blue  Bird  Beauty  Salon,  shared  with  a
barber,  was  located  on  South  Main
Cross.  Her  salon  created  a  good  repu¬
tation.  Mrs.  Natterman  remembered
her  first  cliental,  Mrs.  Demming,
Mrs.  Nell  Glover  ,  Fern  Ward  and
Mrs.  Durr.  From  then  on  these  wo¬
men  were  her  life-long  clients.

In  1924  the  Blue  Bird  Beauty  Salon
moved  to  South  Washington  and  Mrs.

atterman  changed  the  salon  name
to  Wreatha ’s  Harper-Method  Beautv
Salon.  J

In  1935,  Wreath’s  Harper-Method
Beauty  Salon  moved  for  it’s  final  loca¬
tion  in  Mrs.  NaCerrnan’s  home  on  905
West  Poplar.  There  her  salon  opened
early  and  closed  sometimes  in  late
evening.  ‘My  Salon  was  a  very  lively
salon’!  Mrs.  Natterman’s  daughter,
Nancy,  worked  in  the  Salon  with  her
mother  for  25  five  years.  Mrs.  Natter¬
man  has  loved  her  profession.  She  has

by  DENISE  ORR

—  Bre«ze-Courler  writer

had  ‘loyal,  life-long  clients’.  Mrs.  Nat¬
terman  s  salon  has  had  the  privilege
of  a  write-up  in  the  Christian  County
Historical  Book.

Mrs.  Natterman  was  bom  in  1902  in
Taylorville  to  William  and  Ada  May
(Oyler)  Poor.  She  meet  her  husband-
to-be,  Ernest  Natterman,  and  the  cou¬
ple  were  married  September  6, 1925  in
the  home  of*Mr.  tmaTttrSTTOfin^lan^
uer  of  Springfield.  The  ceremony  was
performed  by  the  Reverend  John
Thor  of  Springfield  and  they  were  at¬
tended  by  Mrs.  Emily  Poor  and  the
late  Earl  Poor.  Mr.  Natterman  owned
and  operated  Natterman’s  Service
Station  for  approximately  50  years
Mr.  Natterman  passed  away  on  Jan-
uaty  10, 1978.

Mrs.  Natterman  is  the  mother  ol
one  daughter,  Mrs.  Ronald  fNanr^
She  also  has  thTee"

_  -Z5&

grandchildren,  Steve,  age  25,  attends
DePaul  University  in  Chicago;  £usie
age  22,  is  a  graduate  of  Lincoln  LaBci
Community  College  in  Springfield;
and  Scott,  age  18,  attends  Southern  Il¬
linois  University.

Gordon  K.  Poor,  71,  Retired
Coal  Miner  Of  Edinburg  Dies

Gordon  K.  Poor,  71,  of  Edin¬
burg,  died  at  4:42  Thursday
afternoon  in  St.  Vincent
Memorial  Hospital  where  he
had  been  admitted  on  Wednes¬
day.  He  had  been  in  ill  health
for  three  years.

ye  was  born  in  Christian
County  on  July  26, 1907,  a  son  of
William  W.  and  Ada  Mae  Oyler
Poor  and  was  married  first  to
Alberta  Ft.  Dain,  on  April  1,
1933,  at  Springfield  and  she
preceded  him  in  death  in  1974.
On  September  25,  1975,  he  was
married  to  Marie  Fruit.  He  had
lived  most  all  of  life  in  the
Taylorville  Edinburg  com¬
munities  and  was  a  retired  coal
miner.  He  was  a  member  of  the
Edinburg  Baptist  Church  and
theU.M.W.A.

two  sons,  Malvern  George  of
Boody  and  Ronald  D.jof  Califor¬
nia;  a  daughter,  Mrs.  Briin
(  Elaigfi.)  Webb  of  Okeechobbe,
FliTffour  sisters,  Mrs.  Muriel
Woods  of  Pana;  Mrs.  Wreatha
Natterman,  this  city;  Mrs.
Dorothy  Achenbach  of  Granite
City;  and  Mrs.  Mary  Jones  of
Urbana;  three  brothers,  Glenn
Poor  of  Urbana;  Eldon  Poor  of
this  city;  and  William  Levoy
Poor  of  Decatur;  11  grand¬
children  and  one  great¬
grandchild.

The  body  was  received  by
Connolly  Funeral  Home  where
arrangements  are  incomplete.

Mrs.  Poor

in  Christian  tounty,fhe  <

Mr.  and  Mrs.  Will  Poor,  well
mown  residents  of  this  com¬
munity  today,  are  observing
their  57th  wedding  anniversary
in  their  home  on  Rural  Route  4r
No  formal  celebration  has  been
planned,  but  there  will  be  a  fam¬
ily  gathering  in  their  home  this
evening.

\  The  marriage  of  Mr.  Poor  and
Miss  Ada  Mae  Oyler  took  place
in  Taylorville  on  July  11,  1895.
Their  entire  married  life  has
been  spent  in  Christian  County
where  Mr.  Poor  has  been  en¬
gaged  in  farming.  He  is  81  years
old  and  his  wife  is  76.

They  became  the  parents  of
nine  children,  eight  of  whom  are
living,  namely:  Glenn,  of  Ur-
bana;  Mrs.  Mary  Jones,  Urbana;
Gordon,  of  Edinburg;  Levoy,  of
Decatur;  Mrs.  Dorothy  Achen-
bach,  Granite  City;  Murill  Wood,
of  Witt;  Eldon,  and  Mrs.  Wrea-
tha^atterman,  both  of  this  city.
One  daughter,  Mrs.  Marie  Pear¬
son,  is  deceased.  They  also  have
28  grandchildren  and  14  great
grandchildren.

Former  Local
Resident,  Die

Glenn  Poor  Sr.,  76,  of  Urbana,
a  native  of  this  city,  died  sud¬
denly  Friday,  In  his  home.

Mr.  Poor  was  born  November
9,  1903  at  Taylorville,  a  son  of
William  W.  and  Mae  Oyler
Poor,  and  on  October  8, 1925,  he
was  married  at  Montteello  to
lind  Roberts.  He  was  a
irch  associate  for  the

^Geological  and  Natural  History  -"•SP  _
“leys  at  the  University  of  Il¬
ls,  and  in  1950  he  and  his

aon,  established  Glenn  Poor’s
in  Urbana..  He  was  a
ber  of  the  University  Place

lstian  Church,  Urbana;  Il¬
ls  Society  of  Professional
rs  and  the  Twin  City
io  Club.  For- several  years
-  he  was  active  in  Cub  Scouts  and
.!  Little  League.

Surviving  are  his  widow;

?  three  sons,  Glenn  Jr.,  William,
R.,  both  of  Urbanat  and  James
j-  R.  of  Seymour;  two  daughters,
Barbara  Carpenter  and
atricia  McGinty,  both  oif  *"*
